| [f7913ac553ae](https://github.com/git-df-scott/jacobian_planar/commit/f7913ac553ae0bdf7b15b91cacc679f31aae68a3) | 2026-08-21T17:43:27+00:00 | Claude | A completely different hunt: classify by the deck group instead of the degree pair. A counterexample F gives a degree-mu extension C(x,y)/C(P,Q); every element of its deck group is a birational self-map of C^2 fixing P and Q, and finite-order birational self-maps of the plane are classified. The engine is a one-line lemma: F etale at a fixed point p of a regular sigma makes F injective near p, so sigma = id - hence every nontrivial deck transformation is fixed-point-free on C^2. At mu=2 the extension is automatically Galois, and every class in Bayle-Beauville's classification has a fixed locus meeting C^2: computed explicitly for de Jonquieres (fixed conic c*y^2-2a*y-b=0, whose resultant with the pole locus is (a^2+bc)c, nonzero exactly when sigma is a genuine involution, so sigma is regular along its own fixed curve), and by genus/rationality of the boundary for Geiser and Bertini. So a plane Keller counterexample cannot have geometric degree 2, and more generally cannot be Galois - it is a NON-GALOIS covering of degree mu >= 3. This converges with every known mechanism: Alpoge's dim-3 example has mu=3 exactly, Gallagher realizes every mu>=3, and the tangent sweep of a degree-d curve has mu=d+1 |

Cost: substitution densifies, 8592 -> 1.7M terms, so this is a genuine exact reduction but not automatically a win for downstream solving. |

The campaign closed this case by infeasibility - OOM-killed at 2m46s of a 900s budget on 16GB - and named the fix as more RAM or an F4/FGLM engine such as msolve. Both are the wrong answer: msolve, pointed at it for the first time tonight, SIGSEGVed at 6GB and gave NO VERDICT at 10GB with the machine to itself, and Singular slimgb also gave no verdict. What worked is reading the system's own structure: it contains 6 single-monomial equations, each a product of exactly two variables over 8 distinct variables with c_46 in four of them, and over a field c_i*c_j=0 is not a constraint to grind through but an exhaustive binary split whose sides are both cheap - setting c_46=0 kills four monomial equations outright, while c_46!=0 FORCES all four partners to zero. Because the monomial equations share variables the tree collapses instead of growing: 15 leaves, every one EMPTY. A memory wall is a statement about the root system, and splitting first means the solver never sees the root. Soundness: uv=0 implies u=0 or v=0, so the split is exhaustive and every leaf empty implies the root empty; one unresolved leaf would make the run NO VERDICT and is reported as such. Controls confirm the solver is not a can't-fail certifier - P-POS on a system with known solutions correctly returns NOT EMPTY with 2 lead leaves, P-NEG on a contradictory system correctly returns EMPTY. Scope: one prime, announced not closed. |

The lesson is structural rather than about hardware: a memory wall is a statement about the ROOT system, and a system carrying monomial equations c_i*c_j=0 should be split before it is solved, because over a field that is an exhaustive binary case split whose branches are each far smaller and whose tree collapses when the monomial equations share variables. Scope: one prime for both, announced not closed. |
